Weight-space interpolation \\citep{wortsman2022model} can reduce multiplicity among models of a single architecture; we instead characterize multiplicity across architectures, where interpolation is not applicable. \\citet{jiang2024predictive} extend multiplicity analysis to foundation models in the fine-tuning regime; our work examines the pre-training regime under architectural variation. We show that changing the recurrent backbone---without explicit diversification---is associated with per-example multiplicity that varies systematically by task type. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} characterize examples via training dynamics; our decomposition operates at deployment time across architectures. {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "e97f9548-8005-4e18-bc97-4c7963c2e721",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "7317d232-78be-4d34-bd5d-dc0798b64383",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "ac92ee29-31e6-4e92-a6ca-d5889c452b33",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "LAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).",
    "newString": "LAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~\\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nLAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% BCa confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 17,

          " \\end{itemize}",
          " ",
          "-LAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).",
          "+LAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.",
          " ",

```text
650	
651	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Scale Comparison}
652	
653	Table~\ref{tab:340m_multi_bench} extends the scale comparison to all four main benchmarks. Two patterns emerge. First, $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M across all benchmarks, with the largest gap on ARC-Easy ($+$8.4pp) and the smallest on LAMBADA ($+$0.6pp). Second, pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J}_h = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales); three-architecture results are stable across both 340M and 1.3B scales (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.08--0.10), providing indirect support for scale robustness. This confirms that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---is scale-robust even as its magnitude increases at smaller scale. Notably, the $\alpha$ inflation does not track accuracy loss: HellaSwag has the largest accuracy gap between scales yet the smallest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$1.9pp), while ARC-Easy has a smaller accuracy gap but the largest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$8.4pp), arguing against accuracy as the primary driver of scale-dependent multiplicity.
654	
```

          " ",
          "-Table~\\ref{tab:340m_multi_bench} extends the scale comparison to all four main benchmarks. Two patterns emerge. First, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M across all benchmarks, with the largest gap on ARC-Easy ($+$8.4pp) and the smallest on LAMBADA ($+$0.6pp). Second, pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J}_h = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales); three-architecture results are stable across both 340M and 1.3B scales (Jaccard $\\approx$ 0.08--0.10), providing indirect support for scale robustness. This confirms that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---is scale-robust even as its magnitude increases at smaller scale. Notably, the $\\alpha$ inflation does not track accuracy loss: HellaSwag has the largest accuracy gap between scales yet the smallest $\\Delta\\alpha$ ($+$1.9pp), while ARC-Easy has a smaller accuracy gap but the largest $\\Delta\\alpha$ ($+$8.4pp), arguing against accuracy as the primary driver of scale-dependent multiplicity.",

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{caption}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\setlist[itemize]{itemsep=1pt, parsep=0pt, topsep=2pt}\n\n\\setlength{\\textfloatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\floatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\intextsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\abovecaptionskip}{3pt}\n\\setlength{\\belowcaptionskip}{0pt}\n\\setlength{\\dbltextfloatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\dblfloatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\abovedisplayskip}{4pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\belowdisplayskip}{4pt plus 1pt minus 1pt}\n\\setlength{\\parskip}{0pt plus 0.5pt}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nPractitioners select among hybrid linear recurrent architectures using aggregate benchmarks, but architectures that appear interchangeable by these metrics can differ on which specific examples they get right. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that adding attention is associated with substantially different per-example changes across architectures (Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures). This predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) more than attention dose. Per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention and remains flat, even as aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend on one benchmark (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$). Majority vote fails to exploit the resulting complementarity. Confidence-informed routing extracts 36\\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA ($+5.2$\\,pp), suggesting these differences are partially actionable on high-multiplicity tasks.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run, so architectural and seed-specific effects cannot be fully disentangled. The $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and consistency across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$ is 0.017, so LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ does not reach conventional significance.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\n\nAll models are from the publicly available model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis code and per-example evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run, so architectural and seed-specific effects cannot be fully disentangled. The $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and consistency across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$ is 0.017, so LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ does not reach conventional significance.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.",
          "+\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open; all four per-example benchmarks use discriminative scoring, so multiplicity under open-ended generation is untested. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

### Tool result

_source line 7896 · 2026-05-24T12:32:10.876Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MefzKYRDzREmDvyZQGyxRr`</summary>

```text
653	Table~\ref{tab:340m_multi_bench} extends the scale comparison to all four main benchmarks. Two patterns emerge. First, $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M across all benchmarks, with the largest gap on ARC-Easy ($+$8.4pp) and the smallest on LAMBADA ($+$0.6pp). Second, pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J}_h = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales); three-architecture results are stable across both 340M and 1.3B scales (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.08--0.10), providing indirect support for scale robustness. This suggests that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---is consistent across the two scales examined, even as its magnitude increases at smaller scale. Notably, the $\alpha$ inflation does not track accuracy loss: HellaSwag has the largest accuracy gap between scales yet the smallest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$1.9pp), while ARC-Easy has a smaller accuracy gap but the largest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$8.4pp), arguing against accuracy as the primary driver of scale-dependent multiplicity.
654	
```

</details>

100	\end{figure}

102	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration produces a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
103	
104	\begin{figure}[t]

### Tool result

_source line 7918 · 2026-05-24T12:32:46.725Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014zPiUe1hWHh6ZHxZQioLSr`</summary>

```text
24	    \item Architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---is associated with structured predictive multiplicity ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\leq 0.14$ (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).
25	    \item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\%) across all four benchmarks, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). Minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate variance pattern depends on GDN inclusion (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.
```

</details>

    "newString": "with magnitude that depends on task type rather than attention dose",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GDN, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets. Yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: architectures that score within 1--2 points of each other reach similar accuracy by gaining and losing on different inputs. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance reduction and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (reasoning to retrieval).\n\nSecond, per-example overlap is independent of aggregate variance trends. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows a non-significant decreasing variance pattern ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet even on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---is associated with structured predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). Minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate variance pattern depends on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

```text
71	\subsection{Aggregate Variance and Per-Example Overlap}\label{ssec:decoupling}
72	
73	The per-example findings raise a natural question: do architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics agree on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} shows that only LAMBADA exhibits a monotonically decreasing variance pattern ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered observation ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access.
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
78	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
79	\label{fig:task_specificity}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	\begin{figure*}[t]
83	\centering
84	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
85	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
86	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
87	\end{figure*}
88	
89	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate variance decreases while per-example overlap remains constant as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
90	
91	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1) (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; non-significant), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\bar{J} \approx 0.095$)---each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
92	
93	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_saturation}): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
94	
95	\begin{figure}[t]
```

```text
47	
48	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} The rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.2$\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.2$\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.06$\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).
49	
50	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different formats, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types, confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
```

```text
7	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\% (pure recurrent), 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, and 25\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\%, h12-1 to 7.7\%, and h24-1 to 4\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.
8	
9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
```

    "oldString": "\\caption{Aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}",
    "newString": "\\caption{Descriptive trajectories of aggregate variance and per-example overlap on LAMBADA (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GDN, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets. Yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: architectures that score within 1--2 points of each other reach similar accuracy by gaining and losing on different inputs. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance reduction and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (reasoning to retrieval).\n\nSecond, per-example overlap is independent of aggregate variance trends. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows a non-significant decreasing variance pattern ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet even on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---is associated with structured predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude that depends on task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). Minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate variance pattern depends on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.2$\\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.2$\\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          "+\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.2$\\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.2$\\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison---average-logits ensemble---is left to future work.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different formats, and $\\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types, confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.",

We show that changing the recurrent backbone---without explicit diversification---is associated with per-example multiplicity that varies systematically by task type.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}\nThe design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \\citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \\citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \\citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \\citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \\citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \\citep{fu2023hungry} and Hyena \\citep{poli2023hyena} establish early design points in the hybrid space with gated convolutions and subquadratic operators. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \\citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \\citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \\citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \\citep{basu2026routing}. Recent theoretical work establishes a provable expressiveness hierarchy between hybrid and pure linear attention \\citep{ye2026provable}, providing formal grounding for the architectural differences we characterize at the per-example level. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.\n\n\\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}\nOur three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.\n\n\\paragraph{Underspecification and Predictive Multiplicity.}\n\\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: equivalent aggregate performance can mask per-prediction divergence. NLP instances include seed-dependent BERT disagreement \\citep{mccoy2020berts,sellam2022multiberts,dodge2020finetuning} and four-phase convergence in autoregressive LMs \\citep{fehlauer2025convergence}. \\citet{marx2020predictive} introduced predictive multiplicity as a complementary lens (extending prediction churn; \\citealt{milanifard2016launch}), showing that near-optimal models produce conflicting per-example predictions; extensions address probabilistic classification \\citep{watson2023predictive}, Rashomon capacity \\citep{hsu2022rashomon}, reconciliation \\citep{du2025reconciling,haghighat2026resolving}, and cross-model disagreement as a correctness signal \\citep{gorbett2026crossmodel}. Surveys by \\citet{black2022model} and \\citet{ganesh2025systemizing} map the broader landscape; \\citet{cooper2024arbitrariness} connect arbitrariness to fairness.\n\nThese studies attribute divergence to initialization noise or the near-optimal set within a single architecture. Rashomon-set analyses \\citep{fisher2019all,semenova2022existence,jain2025allocation} examine variation among near-optimal models within a single hypothesis class; ensemble diversity work \\citep{fort2019deep,kuncheva2003measures,brown2005diversity} shows that diverse architectures explore distinct loss-landscape modes. Weight-space interpolation \\citep{wortsman2022model} can reduce multiplicity among models of a single architecture; we instead characterize multiplicity across architectures, where interpolation is not applicable. \\citet{jiang2024predictive} extend multiplicity analysis to foundation models in the fine-tuning regime; our work examines the pre-training regime under architectural variation. We show that changing the recurrent backbone---without explicit diversification---is associated with per-example multiplicity that varies systematically by task type. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} characterize examples via training dynamics; our decomposition operates at deployment time across architectures. Our cross-benchmark variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) argues against single-artifact explanations \\citep{niven2019probing}. The observation that aggregate associations can diverge from individual-level patterns is a well-known statistical phenomenon (ecological fallacy; \\citealt{robinson1950ecological}); our decoupling finding (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) is a controlled instance within recurrent architectural families.\n",

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "14b95e69-2184-4a93-9725-8b27b08b5ba4",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Introduction}\n2\t\n3\tHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GDN, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets. Yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: architectures that score within 1--2 points of each other reach similar accuracy by gaining and losing on different inputs. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n4\t\n5\tThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n6\t\n7\tPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n8\t\n9\tWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.\n10\t\n11\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n12\t\\centering\n13\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n14\t\\caption{Descriptive trajectories of aggregate variance and per-example overlap on LAMBADA (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n15\t\\label{fig:decoupling}\n16\t\\end{figure}\n17\t\n18\tOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (reasoning to retrieval).\n19\t\n20\tSecond, per-example overlap is independent of aggregate variance trends. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows a non-significant decreasing variance pattern ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet even on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n21\t\n22\tOur contributions are as follows:\n23\t\\begin{itemize}\n24\t    \\item Architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---is associated with structured predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude that depends on task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n25\t    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). Minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate variance pattern depends on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n26\t\\end{itemize}"
  },

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}",
    "newString": "% [PLACEHOLDER] Figure 2: Experimental Framework overview\n% Awaiting figure from Main Agent. Insert here with:\n% \\begin{figure}[t]\n% \\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_experimental_framework.pdf}\n% \\caption{...}\n% \\label{fig:experimental_framework}\n% \\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~\\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nLAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% BCa confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}).\n",

9	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.
10

    "oldString": "Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.",
    "newString": "Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: per-example overlap remains flat even as aggregate variance changes.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GDN, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets. Yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: architectures that score within 1--2 points of each other reach similar accuracy by gaining and losing on different inputs. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Descriptive trajectories of aggregate variance and per-example overlap on LAMBADA (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (reasoning to retrieval).\n\nSecond, per-example overlap is independent of aggregate variance trends. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows a non-significant decreasing variance pattern ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet even on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---is associated with structured predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude that depends on task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). Minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate variance pattern depends on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.",
          " ",

Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories.
